**Quarter synthesis:** the debate has shifted from "does AI demand hold?" to "how much margin can TSMC extract" — the flow (Fubon, Redburn, UBS, JPM) is unanimously bullish on 2027 pricing (hikes ≥10% incl. Apple, GM toward 70%) and adds the server-CPU as a new leg. New from 06-26: TSMC's Shanghai Symposium revealed a CoWoS 5.5× reticle at 98% yield + 6T SRAM GAA area win, pointing to higher CoWoS throughput. New from 06-27: **SFG "Feeding the Foundry Beast"** (Hosseini, $432.35, Positive) frames SCA/LTA templates migrating from memory to foundry logic, compressing TSMC's revenue volatility and justifying multiple expansion. New from 06-28: **UBS raises PT to NT$3,400** (from NT$3,000) ahead of the July 16 Q2 print, lifting 2026 growth to 37%, 2027 to 38%, and capex to $60/$80/$95bn; N3 capacity road to 220kwpm and N2 to 160kwpm by 2028E; +5-10% leading-edge pricing in early 2027. Foundry 2.0 data (Counterpoint via Jefferies) confirms TSMC +41% YoY in Q1 2026 with CoWoS still constrained. OpenAI Jalapeno (3nm) + Jalapeno2 (A16+CoWoS) + QCOM Dragonfly/AI300 (3nm/2nm+CoWoS) add incremental demand vectors beyond the NVDA/AVGO/Google anchor. New from 06-29/30: **MS Charlie Chan raises PT to NT$2,888** (from NT$2,588, OW) into the July 16 call (expects capex guide to $56bn, rev to ~40% YoY); the foreign-IB PT table (Aletheia/UBS/Macquarie/CLSA NT$3,330-3,500) sits well above. The Jefferies buy-side GM survey (is 70% too high vs VA 66.9%) frames the key Q2 debate. **Packaging is now a two-front story:** DIGITIMES/@QQ_Timmy flag CoPoS fast-tracked to ~2029 MP (5-6x output), while MS Amir Amerian's glass/panels note + the former-AMD-director expert call bracket the CoWoS-vs-EMIB-T debate (expert: CoWoS still the "gold standard"; EMIB-T early/speculative but performance/cost-advantaged >9.7 reticle). Backfilled from earlier in the quarter: **Barron's "Fab Five" cover (06-01)** puts a hard number on the Taiwan-concentration bear case (~80% of long-term assets) alongside a cheap-growth valuation framing (21x FY27 P/E, 0.58x PEG, +90% EPS by 2027); **SemiAnalysis's HBM deep-dive (08-11)** clarifies TSMC's HBM economics as a **double monetization** — base-die fab (N12/N3, for NVDA/AVGO/hyperscalers) plus CoWoS packaging on the same physical unit after it round-trips through SK Hynix/Micron for TSV+stacking — reinforcing the CoWoS-demand-pull thesis from the memory/AI cycle without TSMC ever holding the TSV/stacking IP itself. New from 06-29/07-01: **UBS (Sunny Lin) raises its TSMC CoWoS capacity forecast to 130k/180k WPM end-2026/27 (from 120k/150k)** — a genuine upward revision on top of the already-bullish JPM (115k/175k/220k) and MS (200kwpm 2027) trajectories — with backend/AP now ~12-15% of sales '26/27E and ~50% CAGR over 5 years; MS separately published a 50-page CoWoS-update report breaking the ramp out by CPU/GPU/ASIC/Optical segment (2026-06-29), and DIGITIMES reports the whole supply chain is under a gag order while TSMC fast-tracks CoWoS and CoPoS (2026-06-30). **Packaging-moat debate deepens:** an AlphaSense expert call (former AMD director) calls CoWoS the clear leader vs Intel's EMIB-T with a 5-10yr-durable moat, while MS's Amir Amerian "glass/panels" sales note surfaces a new angle — TSMC's Glass Core Substrate discussions with Ibiden/Innolux (June 11, Japan) — read as proof-of-concept only, adoption ~2H28, full-year-earnings impact not until 2029. **Market-share/WFE reality check:** a Capstone expert call (former Samsung EVP) puts TSMC leading-edge share at 85-95% (Samsung a distant #2, 10-15% overflow ceiling) and calls the Street's $300bn WFE narrative "completely unrealistic" (sees growth past $200bn, not to the highs, on fab-capacity/power constraints) — a useful bottoms-up check against the sell-side's uniformly bullish capex trajectory elsewhere on this page. Jefferies (Cristina Titu, 07-01) notes shares +3% in Asia as investors position for a possible 70% GM print next quarter, and DIGITIMES separately flags UMC-Intel cooperation rumors (12nm→Intel 3) met with skepticism. **New from 07-02:** UBS's "Sector Keys July 2026" reiterates Buy/PT NT$3,400, restates the N2-to-220kwpm-by-2028 and server-CPU-to-$44bn theses, raises capex to $60/80/95bn and CoWoS to 180/70kwpm (TSMC/OSATs) end-2027, and pegs TSMC at 17x/13x 2027/28E PE vs SOX 34x (+62% YTD) — consolidating the bull case already on the page. Cutting the other way, SemiAnalysis's ECTC 2026 roundup is the cleanest single read on the packaging-competition front: TSMC's microfluidic-cooling demo (5.3kW@8LPM) is a multi-kW-package enabler, but TSMC ran only 3 ECTC papers (vs Intel 12 / Samsung 11) and Intel's EMIB-T is framed as "the most credible alternative to CoWoS" for large-package accelerators — plus a wave of interposer-less/panel-level alternatives (EMIB-T, FO-EB, glass-core, DBrM) all target the circular-wafer size/utilization limits of CoWoS-R/-L (though CoWoS stays ahead on DTC/eDTC + IVR + active LSI and volume). **New from 07-06 (into the July 16 print):** **JPM (Gokul) raises PT to NT$3,100 (from NT$2,500), Overweight**, lifting FY26/27/28E EPS +5%/+10%/+16% and, notably, the **Datacenter-AI revenue CAGR to 69% ('24-29E) from 59% — above TSMC's own mid-to-high-50s guide** (the sell-side now modelling DC-AI *ahead* of management), with 2Q26 GM close to 70% (JPMe 69.5%) and capex raised to $58/$78/$84bn '26/27/28 — a fresh upward mark on both the GM-toward-70% and rising-capex debates. **MS (Charlie Chan)** published a 58-page constructive preview + CoWoS update (CPU/GPU/ASIC/Optical/China AI Compute, Industry View Attractive) into the same print. **Citi (Laura Chen)** adds a Taiwan design-service read-through (prefers MediaTek/GUC for AI-ASIC growth into 2027; foundry/advanced-packaging/HBM/ABF supply "remains tight") supportive of TSMC's ASIC/CoWoS demand. **Bernstein (David Dai)** reiterated OP/US$430 (NT$2,780; 20x forward Q5-8 EPS NT$139) inside its ASML High-NA deep-dive, framing **TSMC as the slowest leading-edge logic player to adopt High-NA (~2030, A10)** — behind Intel (2028) and Samsung (~2029) and after DRAM (~2027) — reading TSMC's "HNA too expensive" stance as prudence (wait for availability/throughput to lower cost-per-exposure) rather than a rejection; a near-term litho-cost positive but a process-leadership tell that rivals adopt HNA first. Net: the desk is uniformly bullish into July 16 on GM (~70%), capex (rising) and DC-AI demand (now modelled above management's guide). **07-07:** UBS's SpaceX/SPCX-initiation "Terafab" note is a mixed read-through — Musk is grateful to TSMC but wants it to expand faster than TSMC is comfortable with ("we either build the terafab or we don't have the chips"), and UBS thinks Terafab could reach TSMC-scale WFE within 4-5yrs (a potential new >$50B/yr WFE spender). Near-term it corroborates the capacity-can't-keep-up-with-AI pricing tailwind, but it flags a credible new leading-foundry-scale entrant as a long-dated competitive risk on top of the (still dominant) share/moat picture elsewhere on this page. **07-08 (into the July 16 print):** Fubon's "Expecting GM upside going forward" preview is the cleanest bull-side setup mark — **Buy reiterated, PT NT$3,500 (top pick)**, 2Q26 on track to beat (GM ~67.1%), 3Q26 guide expected to beat (+10-12% QoQ vs cons HSD%), FY26 guide "very likely" raised again to +30-35% YoY, and FY27 GM pushed toward the 70% benchmark on a widening 15-20% S/D gap + 10%-for-all wafer hikes incl. Apple (FY26/27 EPS NT$103.90/144.13, market-high vs cons NT$99.52/127.84). Two new datapoints: **Arizona phase-1 GM has already reached ~50% at full UTR / 90% yield — faster than expected** (a direct rebuttal to the overseas-dilution bear point, corroborating the earlier 06-05 Fubon "AZ P1 ~50% after 2 yrs" read), and **Google's planned EMIB-T for TPU V9 (late-27/28) likely falls back to TSMC's 2028 CoWoS** as Intel struggles to hit the 95%+ yield Google requested by mid-2027 — feeding Fubon's total-CoWoS 210-220kwpm end-2028 forecast (a positive CoWoS read layered on top of the UBS/JPM/MS/Nomura capacity-raise consensus). **07-08/07-09 (final setup into July 16):** a **JPM (Gokul) 2Q26-preview expert call** puts 2Q26 GM at **~69.5-70% vs guide ~67.5%** (above buy-side, esp. overseas near mid-60s) but flags it as a **short-lived** catalyst, with 3Q26 GM slightly lower QoQ (N2 + overseas dilution, offset by N3 turning accretive in 2H26); the fresh marks are **2027 blended ASP +25-26% YoY** (broad ~10% N2/N3 hikes), **N3 ramping faster to 240-250kwpm by 2029-30 → 10-16% EPS upside for '27/'28**, **DC-AI toward ~70% of total by 2029**, FY26 capex stable $56-58B but **no concrete 2027 numeric capex guide likely (a possible disappointment for US investors)**, aggressive competitive pushback (90-95% N2 share), and **75-80% of new-merchant-CPU CoWoS (AMD Venice/NVDA Vera) shifting to OSATs (ASE/Amkor)** — an OSAT tailwind and an in-house-CoWoS-freeing read. **GS (Evelyn Yu, 07-09)** reiterates the "stronger growth trajectory" theme (TSMC accelerating the capacity build, MediaTek riding AI-ASIC momentum). Demand corroboration: **@QQ_Timmy's June GB NVL72 checks** (~8,000 racks in June: Foxconn ~3,300, Quanta ~2,300-2,400, Wistron ~1,200-1,300; brokers holding ~7-8k/mo) and a moat framing that TSMC's **EDA/IP ecosystem (OIP)** is the underrated pillar beyond PPA/EUV/yield. **DIGITIMES (07-09)** adds that AI demand is pushing TSMC + Samsung to raise foundry prices (with Rapidus aiming to undercut on 2nm) — corroborating the 2027 price-hike thesis. Net: the pre-print flow stays uniformly bullish on GM (~70%), pricing (broad 2027 hikes) and DC-AI mix, with the only cautionary notes being the "short-lived-catalyst" GM framing and the risk that TSMC declines to hand US investors a 2027 capex number on July 16. **The single dissenting rating arrives 07-09 from TD Cowen (Krish Sankar): Hold, PT $400 on the TSM-US ADR — ~8% BELOW the $436.98 spot and the only downside-implying rating on the page** — even though its capex assumptions ($56-60/$80/$100B for '26/'27/'28) are the most aggressive here and its EUV read (38 of 88 ASML tools in 2027, below its ~45-tool demand) reinforces the '27 EUV-ceiling debate; i.e. the caution is a valuation/multiple call on an ADR that has already run, not a demand or margin call. **07-09 (GS webinar detail):** Goldman's Evelyn Yu webinar fills in the specifics behind its "stronger growth trajectory" framing — **FY27 capex raised to $78bn (from $70bn)**, FY26 top-line **~40% YoY** (vs the >30% guide), **2Q26 GM ~68.5% toward 70%**, and the most aggressive CoWoS mark on the page: **pure CoWoS ~220kwpm by end-2027 (from ~130k end-2026), or ~280kwpm incl WMC**, raised +30k on the Nvidia Vera CPU — vs JPM/Gokul's ~175k (upside ~200k, mostly OSATs). The two brokers **converge on $78bn FY27 capex** but **diverge on CoWoS-27 (GS 220k vs JPM 175-200k)** — divergence kept, not reconciled. GS frames rush/hot-run/super-hot-run at **~10-20% of ≤5nm output at a 50-100% premium** and a **mid-single-digit 2027 hike with upside** ("similar to COVID-era" tightness) as the core GM levers. Filled-in file-1 detail (Gokul expert call): N3 utilization **~125-140% incl cross-fab back-end (N7/N12)**, ~600k/25-30% N3 gap, **no 2H26 baseline hike** (only hot/super-hot-run), mature 12" not shrinking, wafer-out ~170k→240-250k N-adv end-28→29-30. **07-11 (Susquehanna Technology Hardware Weekly, Mehdi Hosseini):** SIG adds a fresh advanced-packaging frame — CoWoS ~130K→195K wpm (YE26→YE28), SoIC ramping through 2028, adv-packaging $/wafer $5→$8 (2021-24→2025-28) — and the sharpest COUPE read on the page: **COUPE stays FAU-constrained through YE27** (PIC <0.5K→~5-10K wpm; FAU holds COUPE shipments <0.5M units/qtr, a Spectrum-X yield constraint), so the high-volume CPO ramp is a **2028** story and the near-term supply-chain leverage sits in the **FAU layer** — where GLW's glass/FAU stack (co-developed w/ GlobalFoundries) is the alternative to CoWoS-L/COUPE (see [[GLW]]). **07-14:** a Mirae Asset SK Hynix note flags **TSMC's record June rev NT$443bn (+68% YoY)** and a **2Q26 revenue-guide beat** as evidence the AI capex backdrop stays strong (combined big-tech/neocloud backlog $2.1tr, +24% QoQ; 2-yr-recognition $656bn, +38% QoQ → higher hyperscaler capex "all but inevitable") — a light read-through into the 07-16 print. This corroborates @dnystedt's 07-10 rumor of **≥200K CoWoS wpm by 2027** (conservative), while SIG's event calendar pins the ASML (Jul 15) and TSMC (Jul 16) prints. **Into the 07-16 print (07-13 → 07-15):** the primary June-revenue print (@jukan05/@QQ_Timmy) landed at **NT$442.68bn (+67.9% YoY), a record** and the first June MoM increase since 2021 — implying **Q2 ~US$40.2bn, ~US$0.1bn above the guidance high end** (H1 already above full-year 2022; run-rate ~US$167bn/+37% FY26). The **media consensus bar** (@dnystedt, 07-15) frames the call: **net profit +59% YoY to NT$632.6bn (US$19.6bn, 10th straight up quarter), GM possibly >68%, op-margin/R&D at new highs, Q3 guide +9–12% QoQ, FY likely raised to mid-30s% (bull ~40%)**, and the key upside watch is whether TSMC **lifts the 2024–29 AI-semi revenue CAGR to 70–80% (from mid-to-high-50s)**. The one live tension is **capex** — BofA/Josh Evans flags a "likely capex raise 7/16," but @dnystedt/JPM expect TSMC **not** to hand out a hard 2026/2027 number (may only frame the 3-year plan), the key read-through for [[ASML]]. Demand corroboration: **KeyBanc raised gen-purpose server-CPU unit growth to +30% (from +18.5%)**, with AMD taking incremental TSMC N4 wafers freed by MediaTek smartphone-build cuts and NVDA lifting Vera CPU capacity to 2.5–3.0M units — reinforcing the server-CPU leg. On competition, two 07-15 reads net out roughly neutral-to-positive: **Samsung is sounding out DSPs to outsource the back-end design of Google's 2nm TPU I/O die**, but the TPU compute die stays on **TSMC 1.4nm** and Samsung's 2nm wins are explicitly framed as **overflow "TSMC cannot accommodate"** (a capacity-tightness tell); and **Intel is pulling most of Nova Lake back in-house** (80–90% of tiles vs a prior 60–70%-to-TSMC plan) on 18A yield gains — a modest offset to the Intel-outsourcing thesis. Also (07-13): MS's CPO note lists TSM among the silicon-photonics foundries ($TSM/$GFS/$TSEM), corroborating COUPE positioning. **Bogeys / day-1 thresholds (07-13/07-14):** MS's sales desk (Amir Amerian) is explicit that **expectations are not low** — **2Q GM needs ≥69%** (vs the 67.5% guide high-end), **3Q rev-guide midpoint ≥ low-teens % QoQ (if not +15%)**, and — the swing metric — the **full-year rev-growth guide must go to "at least high-30s"** (buy-side already expects ~40%) for the stock to react positively; JPM (Gokul, 07-13) similarly expects **2Q sales at the guide high-end with GM likely to beat**, and MS (Charlie Chan, 07-14) reiterated **OW** alongside a 60-page CoWoS-update preview. Net: the setup is unambiguously bullish on the fundamentals, but the day-1 reaction hinges on clearing an already-elevated bar (≥69% 2Q GM + a high-30s FY guide). **POST-PRINT (07-16) — the window resolved:** a beat on revenue, FY-guide and capex, but a 3Q GM guide (65–67%) below the ~68% bar, so it reads as "beat-but-margin-debate." 2Q GM **67.7%** cleared the 67.5% guide yet **missed the ≥69% day-1 bogey**; the FY26 revenue guide was raised to **"slightly above 40%"** (cleared the "high-30s a must" swing metric); FY26 capex was reportedly lifted to **US$60–64bn** (BofA + a Bloomberg call-transcript reading — above the ~US$58bn bogey); N2 is already **3% of wafer revenue**. The margin debate (3Q dilution from N2 + overseas fabs) is now the near-term swing factor. See **Current state (Q2 FY26)** for the full scorecard and primary-source detail.

## Intra-quarter — Q3 FY26 window (post-2Q26 print, opens 2026-07-16)
_Broker reactions to the 2Q26 print / earnings call; the next /wiki-ingest run continues this window._

| **08-31** | 🔴🔴 **BofA · Haas Liu** — *"SEMICON Taiwan: proliferating AI boosting the manufacturing innovation"* · **BUY maintained, PO NT$3,100** (20x '27 P/E), px NT$2,420 | demanda / produto / supply | **bull** | ★ 🔴🔴 **THE HEADLINE NUMBER IS A DATACENTRE POWER FORECAST FROM TSMC ITSELF, AND IT IS THE MOST QUOTABLE DEMAND ANCHOR OF THE WEEK: *"At the session in IC Forum, TSMC shared its view that the **data center power capacity will ACCELERATE FROM 5-6GW PER YEAR from '15-24 to 30-40GW PER YEAR from '25-30, reaching a 300GW INSTALLED BASE by '30**, as AI training scales toward multimodal (**5x per annum LLM compute growth**)."*** ➜ **A 6-7x step in the annual build RATE, from the company with the best forward visibility in the industry. ⚠️ Note what it is and is not: an INSTALLED-BASE power forecast, not a capex forecast and not a TSMC revenue forecast. It is the denominator for every per-GW figure on this wiki — including JPM's *"over US$40bn of revenue opportunity per GW for Vera Rubin vs US$25bn for Grace Blackwell"* (2026-08-31), which at 30-40GW/yr implies an enormous silicon pool but must never be multiplied out casually against an installed base.**
🔴 **THE INFERENCE SIDE, WITH A COMPOUNDING RATE: *"it sees **robust AI inference demand, with token consumption growing ~500x SINCE '22** led by **agents and reasoning**, and further compounding by **24x to 120 QUADRILLION TOKENS BY '30**."*** ➜ **~500x realised since 2022 and 24x more to come is the clearest official statement anywhere on this wiki that the workload mix has shifted from training to agentic inference. Cross-file to [themes/ai-inference-demand](themes/ai-inference-demand.md).**
🔴🔴 **THE ROADMAP DISCLOSURES, AND THREE OF THEM ARE NET-NEW TO THIS PAGE: *"TSMC has AI platform across logic (**A13, A12, N2U**). Si-technology for **HBM scaling (from N12 for HBM4 to N3 for HBM5)** and system integration (e.g. **SoIC with 11 TAPE-OUTS for GPU/CPU by 4Q26** and **>14x RETICLE-SIZE CoWoS by '29**) and compute fabric (e.g. CPO)."*** ➜ **(a) **A13 and A12** extend the published logic roadmap beyond A14/A16. (b) **HBM base-die silicon moving N12 (HBM4) → N3 (HBM5)** is the first explicit node path for TSMC's HBM logic content on this page — and it is exactly the business [[SKHYNIX]] is reportedly evaluating [[INTC]] for (*"SK hynix Reportedly Weighs Intel for HBM4E Base Dies amid TSMC COST PRESSURE and Supply Diversification"*, UBS TMI, 2026-08-31). **TSMC publicly claiming the HBM base die in the same week a top customer is reported to be shopping it is the sharpest competitive juxtaposition of the week — logged on both pages, unresolved.** (c) **>14x reticle CoWoS by '29** and **11 SoIC tape-outs by 4Q26** are the hard capacity/adoption markers to track the packaging roadmap against — set against [[MEDIATEK]]'s **9+x reticle** EMIB-T packages for TPU v9 and MS Asia's **">12 reticles"** for TPU v10.**
🔴 **SILICON PHOTONICS — TSMC QUANTIFIES THE I/O GAP THAT MAKES OPTICAL COMPULSORY, AND THE RATIO IS THE USEFUL PART: *"TSMC believes the **optical I/O is becoming PREREQUISITE for AI/HPC system as the I/O GAP IS WIDENING between the compute scaling (3x EVERY 2 YEARS) and I/O scaling (1.4x EVERY TWO YEARS)**. Its **COUPE platform** and a broad photonics device/PDK library facilitate its customer designs… through two complementary paths - **'faster and narrowing'** with higher per-lane speed (**200Gbps to 400Gbps**) and lane count (**128+ lanes**) and **'slower and wider'** by increasing wavelengths from **1 to 16+ through WDM**. To allow further production ramp and wider adoption, TSMC has **lowered COUPE grating coupler insertion loss while improving edge coupler performance** and sees **WAFER-LEVEL TESTING IS CRITICAL to reduce the wasted packaging cost**."*** ➜ **3x vs 1.4x per two years is a compounding divergence — it is the physics argument for CPO/NPO stated as a ratio rather than as a roadmap date, and it is the best single justification on this wiki for the optical names' 2027-28 content step ([[LITE]], [[COHR]], [[CRDO]], [[AAOI]], [[ALAB]]). **The wafer-level-testing point is the underrated one: KGD screening before packaging is where the CPO cost curve actually breaks.** Corroborated the same day by the trade press: *"**TSMC sees SiPh going mainstream in 2027**"* (Digitimes, 2026-08-31), and by the corpus from the equipment side — Taiwan's Kao Ming Tieh (4573) showing a production-grade **120-channel FAU-PIC automated alignment platform** with **Known-Good-Die screening before packaging** at SEMICON, its 1.6T coupling equipment *"validated by multiple silicon-photonics module makers and formally delivered"*, positioned as a standard **CPO alignment (AA Cell) station** (@QQ_Timmy, corpus, 2026-08-30).**
**VALUATION: *"We maintain our Buy rating with **PO at NT$3,100 on 20x '27 P/E**. The share valuation is **undemanding at 16x '27 P/E** on strong technology leadership and extending AI visibility."*** ⚠️ **The whole PO is a 4-turn re-rating on unchanged earnings — logged as such.** **Context: *"SEMICON Taiwan kicked off on August 31st with **IC Forum and Silicon Photonics Summit**, both featuring TSMC's presentations."* Per UBS TMI the same day, this year's exhibition is *"the LARGEST ON RECORD, with its focus expanding to encompass the data center ecosystem led by cloud service providers"*, and **Google's AI Chief Technologist makes his Asia keynote debut.** (BofA Securities · Haas Liu, 2026-08-31) |

It is CONTINUOUS, not periodic: "it's not like we sit down once a year or twice a year."** 🔴 **(3) WHY AI CANNOT BE TRIANGULATED THE OLD WAY — the cleanest statement of this anywhere on the wiki: "those past trends are unit based. You're trying to solve for a total unit. Compute, and computation, THERE'S NO UNIT. IT'S INFINITE."** 🔴 **(4) THE DISCIPLINE TEST, relaying C.C. Wei: "No CEO says, I'm the loser, forget about me… they all come to us. They say they need capacity… EVERYONE IS TELLING THE TRUTH. But WHEN YOU ADD ALL THESE TRUTHS UP, IT MAY NOT BE SO TRUTHFUL, and that's why you have to make your own judgment."** And on sizing: *"considering the five of you on this call, all compete fiercely head to head in the same [end] market… **probably is not going to be 500**."* **The symmetry constraint on the haircut is stated too: "it's not like customers come to us, say need a 100 and we say… let's just do 50. It's good for TSMC. We're well protected, but too bad for the customer. No, we don't — despite what our competitors say… But at the same time, we have a responsibility to ourselves and our shareholders that we cannot just build to what all the customers want."** 🔴 **(5) THE TIGHTNESS HAS MIGRATED — the single most useful operational datapoint on the call: "the last few years with the rise of AI… more of the tightness has been concentrated on… advanced packaging or CoWoS… NOW, TODAY, MORE OF THE TIGHTNESS IS… ON THE FRONT-END WAFER SIDE."** And on whether wafers are queuing for CoWoS slots: **"do we see a mismatch, inventory, [pile-up]? NO."** ⚠️ **No CoWoS lead time, queue length or capacity figure was given — the question was asked and NOT answered quantitatively.** 🔴 **(6) THE THREE CAPACITY LEVERS, because new fabs take 2-3 years and "no one can pump out 20 fabs in one year": new capex · TOOL CONVERSION, framed as a moat — "we are converting, for example, 5 nanometer tools to support 3 nanometer capacity… we're taking parts of OTHER NODES to support the strong demand for three. THIS IS SOMETHING THAT MY COMPETITORS CANNOT DO" · FAB PRODUCTIVITY, partly via internal AI, "to generate what we call more wafer output."** **Greenfield-led strategy UNCHANGED (Carla's question), and no capacity number offered: "I don't have a capacity number to give you."** **On expansion risk the word is deliberately "constraint," not "bottleneck" — land, shell, construction workers, tool vendors — but "our CFO [said] we don't expect any bottlenecks to our expansion."** |
| 08-18 | 🔴🔴 **MANAGEMENT — same call · THE THREE STRUCTURAL POLICY DISCLOSURES (no SCAs · small-customer allocation · packaging is strategic not financial)** | capital / competicao / margem | **bull** | ★★ **These are the parts that change how the equity should be modelled, not just how it is described.** 🔴 **(A) NO LONG-TERM CONTRACTS / SCAs — EVER. "Would we consider, like [the] memory guys, long term contracts, or agreements, or SCAs? THAT WE WILL NOT."** The reasoning is a trust argument, and he concedes the bargaining power he is declining to use: *"today, my capacity is really tight… **I could get you all on this call to sign anything I want.** Like, okay, sign it. **But unless you think it's a super duper cycle forever** — we have convictions in the mega trend, **but it's still cyclical**. So what happens when things are less tight? You're already suffering. You have high inventory. I come to you waving a piece of paper… **I'm hurting you while you're already down**… how are you gonna feel about TSMC as a trustable partner in the mid to long term?"* **What IS available: "we have taken prepayments in the past — MORE SO ON CUSTOMERS' INSISTENCE… there are ways we can discuss on risk sharing with customers, and indeed, we are." On other mechanics: "I cannot [share], because that's between ourselves and our customers." On performance to date: "so far so good."** 🔴 **(B) AT MAXIMUM TIGHTNESS, SMALL CUSTOMERS GET PROTECTED — attributed to a RECENT INTERNAL C.C. WEI DIRECTIVE: "when the capacity is so tight, we have to… especially make sure we take care of the SMALLER customers. Because 50 wafers to the large customer, maybe not that big a deal, but for the small customer, IT'S A MATTER OF SURVIVAL. And why do we do it? It's also selfish, because we have seen too many times that the small customer today becomes the mid to large customer tomorrow."** ⚠️ **A policy statement, not a quantified reservation — but it is a real modelling caveat against assuming [[NVDA]]/[[AVGO]] simply absorb all incremental leading-edge output.** **Allocation process otherwise: transparent per-customer commitments ("no one is trying to play tricks or pull the rug out at the last minute"), larger customers naturally get larger allocation, but "contrary to what our competitors might say, we don't pick and choose or play favorites… we have like 538 customers"** (given twice as "530 plus" and "like 538"). 🔴 **(C) ADVANCED PACKAGING IS "STRATEGIC, NOT FINANCIAL": "the significance of it is strategic. NOT financial. The importance of advanced packaging for TSMC, CC said, is to enable simply MORE LOGIC WAFER MANUFACTURING BUSINESS… No matter which way you cut it — capex-wise, revenue-wise, most importantly PROFITABILITY-wise — where do we make our money? Packaging? No. Manufacturing semiconductor logic wafers."** **Which is why outsourcing is welcomed rather than resisted — ASE and Amkor as partners, and on [[INTC]] EMIB: "it looks good on paper… the trick is you need to deliver it in real world production, but it doesn't matter — [if] they can do it, WE WELCOME IT. Because that helps our customers out on the packaging side, and that's gonna mean more wafer business for us."** ⚠️ **The one claim TSMC explicitly disputes is packaging as an on-ramp to foundry — see the [[INTC]] and [[SAMSUNG]] pages for the full rebuttal.** |
| 08-18 | 🔴🔴 **MANAGEMENT — same call · POWER AND ELECTRICITY IS THE #1 FORECASTING INPUT TO THE END OF THE DECADE, AND TSMC HAS SENT TEAMS TO THE US TO CHECK IT** | demanda / supply | **bull on the finding, but it names TSMC's own capex-cut trigger** | ★★ **THE HIGHEST-VALUE ITEM ON THE CALL, AND IT IS A NEW DISCLOSURE.** *"In our planning and forecasting there's a lot of things you need to look at, but looking out through the end of the decade, **THE KEY THING WE FOCUS ON IS POWER AND ELECTRICITY**."* **The mechanism, stated with gestures: chip demand from customers to the end of the decade is "super strong, super robust", "but… what about the power and electricity? NOT A PROBLEM TODAY, NOT A PROBLEM TOMORROW. But what about 3 to 5 years from now? WHAT IF THE POWER [AND] ELECTRICITY IS ONLY HERE [and] THE CHIP DEMAND IS HERE — then why bother?… if you don't consider it, YOU'RE GOING TO BUILD TOO MUCH AND BE LEFT HOLDING THE BAG."** 🔴 **THE ACTION, AND THE RESULT: "one of the things we've been doing recently — customers, customers' customers, but also SENDING TEAMS TO THE US, TO CHECK THE PROGRESS OF THE DATA CENTER BUILD OUT AND THE POWER [AND] ELECTRICITY SITUATION. And so far, the report back is actually QUITE IMPRESSIVE — that the CSPs have been well prepared to work on this. And will continue to check that."** **Why this matters more than any sell-side power framework on the wiki: the most capital-at-risk party in the chain is physically auditing its customers' customers' interconnects, has told you unprompted that this is the variable it would cut capex on, and has reported back POSITIVE. That is a 2028-30 capex-durability datapoint no broker model currently carries.** 🔴 **THE FUNDING FILTER — and it excludes part of the demand stack the Street adds in: TSMC underwrites "healthy and wealthy" counterparties, "i.e. the CSPs." "We look at these FOUR HYPERSCALERS. CC said they're rich. THEY'RE EVEN BETTER THAN US AND MAKING MONEY. THEY'RE ALL AHEAD. And by the way, they're making money on ALREADY EXISTING AND REAL BUSINESSES, NOT THE HOPE AND DREAM OF THE FUTURE."** Secondary test: *"when we talk to these CSPs, they can also demonstrate to us in their core verticals — search, advertising, the cloud business — that they're making more money FROM AI."* **And the explicit line drawn: "Some of you are healthy and wealthy CSPs. Some of you may be frontier [labs], some of you neoclouds — the opportunity is there for everyone. But… by 3 to 5 years from now, NOT ALL OF YOU IS GOING TO BE THE WINNER. There are winners and losers."** 🔴 **Read against §1: TSMC weights the CSPs and does NOT underwrite frontier-lab or neocloud commitments — so any model that adds [[CRWV]]/[[NBIS]]/[[OPENAI]]/[[ANTHROPIC]] compute commitments on top of hyperscaler capex to derive TSMC revenue is double-counting demand TSMC itself discounts.** ⚠️ **ON MEMORY AS A GATE TO THE BUILDOUT, ASKED DIRECTLY, THE ANSWER WAS A NON-ANSWER: "Memory and things like that, certainly we watch. Everyone has seen the huge spikes in memory price creating UNNATURAL DISTORTION. And so I think customers are working on it." That is the entire answer — no view on HBM supply, no view on whether memory gates the [[NVDA]] ramp, no quantification.** **On tool supply against competitors bidding up EUV: the answer is a flat yes, on counterparty-durability grounds — see the [[ASML]] page.** **Architecture-agnosticism restated: GPU vs ASIC, training vs inference, fabless vs system vs IDM vs CSP — "ALL ROADS LEAD TO TSMC", resting on two stated assumptions: continued technology leadership, and "don't suddenly turn stupid and introduce TSMC AI chips that compete or conflict with you."** |
| 08-05 | **UBS · Timothy Arcuri ([[AMD]] post-print callback, hosted by Robert Ruple) — a customer-attribution claim on the capex raise** | demanda | **bull** | 🔴 **The single most specific attribution of TSMC's 2026 capex raise on this page, and it names one customer: "ALMOST ALL OF THE RECENT TSMC CAPEX RAISE IS N2-RELATED AND LARGELY TO SUPPORT AMD. LISA WENT THERE THREE TIMES IN THE LAST, I DON'T KNOW, FOUR MONTHS. And so I think that they're finally getting A HUGE INFLECTION IN N2 ALLOCATION."** ➤ **He uses it as the reason he is at >2x for AMD's 2027 server CPU against the company's own "+70%" — i.e. the capacity is visible in the allocation before it is visible in the guide. For this page it is a demand-composition datapoint: it argues the US$60-64bn FY26 capex raise is skewed to N2 for a specific compute customer rather than spread across the AI complex.** ⚠️ **It is an analyst's supply-chain inference, not a TSMC or AMD disclosure, and it is in tension with the N2 customer mix modelled elsewhere on this page ([[AAPL]] A20, Qualcomm, Intel Nova Lake all cited as N2 by Bernstein on 08-10). Carried as a claim with its basis attached, NOT folded into the N2 bottoms-up model.** ⚠️ Machine transcript. (UBS · Timothy Arcuri, hosted by Robert Ruple, 2026-08-05) [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_08_05_arcuri_follow_up_on_amd.html) |
| 07-28 | **[[INTC]] IR · John Pitzer (VP, Investor Relations), BofA-hosted call — PRIMARY TRANSCRIPT; customer-side read-through only, no TSMC rating or PT** | demanda | **bull** | **First-party confirmation from the customer that Intel's outsourcing to TSMC is NOT being wound down as the internal nodes ramp — which is the assumption embedded in most 14A/18A bull cases and is the exact question Bernstein sizes from the other side on 08-10 (Intel ≈ mid-30s% of TSMC's US$15-16bn CPU book in 2025).** **Verbatim: "our THIRD PARTY FOUNDRIES ARE REALLY IMPORTANT PARTNERS. We've got strong relationships with both TSMC, SAMSUNG and quite frankly UMC. And THOSE RELATIONSHIPS WILL CONTINUE TO BE IMPORTANT. I love the fact that TSMC'S BUILDING A LOT OF CAPACITY IN THE US BECAUSE WE NEED WAFERS FROM THEM TO SUPPORT OUR BUSINESS ON THE PC FRONT."** ➤ **Two structural points for the demand model. (1) The dependency is CLIENT-side and persistent: "because you use more external wafers in client… servers you'll do all internally" — Panther Lake still has external tiles, and "as you move to NOVA LAKE, we're STILL GOING TO HAVE SUPPORT OF THE EXTERNAL FOUNDRIES ON THE NOVA LAKE PRODUCT AS WELL." (2) The internalisation target is bounded and confirmed: "we said explicitly with [Nova] Lake that we think 70% OF THE TILES WILL BE INTERNAL. That's still the right ballpark" — on VOLUME SKUs, with the mix varying by SKU, "and that's still the right way to think about [Nova Lake] as well."** ⚠️ **So even on Intel's own plan, ~30% of Nova Lake tile content stays external — and the stated reason for wanting TSMC's Arizona capacity is US supply for PCs, not a temporary bridge. Read this as a floor under the Intel line of TSMC's CPU book through the Nova Lake generation, and hold it against Bernstein's 08-10 claim that "SOME OF INTEL'S UPCOMING CPU NOVA LAKE WILL BE ON TSMC N2 TOO."** **Adjacent, and relevant to the advanced-packaging competitive line on this page: Intel puts its own advanced-packaging capital intensity at "for every DOLLAR you spend on ADVANCED PACKAGING, you're probably spending $5 ON WAFERS," says it can ramp MULTIPLE external EMIB customers in 2027, and names the binding constraint as "NOT INTERNAL — IT'S THE SUBSTRATES EXTERNALLY."** ⚠️ **Also worth carrying for the ASIC/design-services thread: Intel's ASIC business (US$1bn → US$2bn run-rate → "$4bn pretty quickly") is explicitly run on "the CLASSIC [[AVGO]] AND [[MRVL]] MODEL, where WE PROVIDE THE DESIGN SERVICES AND THE WAFERS ARE BUILT AT TSMC" — i.e. a portion of Intel's fastest-growing foundry-adjacent line lands as TSMC wafer demand, not as TSMC competition.** ⚠️ Machine transcript; ASR renders "ASIC" as "A six" and EMIB as "EIP". (Intel IR · John Pitzer, hosted by BofA · Vivek Arya, 2026-07-28) [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_28_intc_ir_post_2q_bofa.html) |
| 07-30 | Bernstein · David Dai / Carmine Milano / Juho Hwang ("ASML: Best Idea 3Q26") | produto | mixed | **Read-through only — an [[ASML]] Best-Idea note with no TSMC rating or PT. Two items, and they point in opposite directions.** **① High-NA: Bernstein has TSMC as the SLOWEST adopter of the leading-edge cohort, and says so explicitly — "TSMC appears likely to delay broad HNA insertion until around 10A, after prioritizing R&D first and extending LNA for earlier nodes."** The adoption roadmap (Exhibit 10) puts **[[INTC]] validated in production at 18A today, broader logic adoption at Intel A14 and [[SAMSUNG]] SF1.4, DRAM at [[SKHYNIX]]/Samsung 1d — and TSMC only at ~A10 at the end of the decade.** ⚠️ **Read both ways, and the page should not simply treat this as bearish.** It is a competitive-timing risk if High-NA proves to be a genuine node-enabling advantage (Bernstein expects adoption by "virtually all leading edge players in DRAM and logic by 2030," i.e. an eventual industry standard, and separately notes TSMC has publicly called High-NA too expensive). But it is equally consistent with TSMC's actual playbook of extending a proven tool generation and inserting new litho only when the cost-per-die math closes — and the note's own economics support the caution: **HNA's advantage is weakest in logic, because "DRAM dies are smaller than logic dies and therefore do not require stitching,"** so logic pays a throughput and cost-per-exposure penalty DRAM does not. TSMC also has the least need to buy process advantage. Litho-intensity context for the node path: **logic intensity peaked >35% at 5nm/3nm, fell to ~32% at 2nm as GAA added non-litho steps, and Bernstein expects it to hold or rise at 1.4nm with HNA and rise again at 1nm.** **② The equipment-cost line is the one that touches the GM debate directly: "Recent commentary from both TSMC and Intel also appears to acknowledge ongoing equipment price inflation"** — Bernstein uses TSMC's own words as corroboration that ASML's pricing lever is real, modelling **EUV ASPs +~10% in 2027 and +7% in 2028 before any like-for-like hikes**, and arguing the like-for-like leg is entirely absent from consensus. That is the vendor-side statement of the same tool-price inflation TSMC cited when raising FY26 capex to US$60-64bn (07-16 call, logged above) and that Fubon sized at ~10% (07-17) — worth holding as an independent confirmation that the inflation is structural and, on the seller's telling, has further to run. (Bernstein · David Dai, 2026-07-30) [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/BERN_258544.html) |

| 07-26 | UBS · Sunny Lin / Randy Abrams / Nicolas Gaudois / Timothy Arcuri ("Can Intel challenge TSMC's advanced packaging leadership?") | supply | **bull** | 🔴 **The first full sell-side stress test of the EMIB-T threat, and it concludes TSMC wins even in the bull case for Intel. Buy retained.** **Market sizing (net-new): advanced-packaging TAM US$19bn (2026E) → US$122bn (2030E), a 59% CAGR.** UBS's **base case** — Intel takes "a meaningful **13% industry share, or US$16bn of sales by 2030, equivalent to packages for ~24mn accelerator dies**," with CoPoS entering mass production for **Nvidia in 2028 and for others in 2029-30** — still leaves **TSMC advanced-packaging sales compounding at a 50% CAGR 2026-30, holding 65-70% share, and advanced packaging rising to 18% of TSMC sales in 2030E from 9% in 2026E.** UBS's **lower case** (stronger EMIB-T adoption) only softens that to a **47% CAGR, 62% share, and 16% of sales**. That is a **~2pt-of-sales-mix difference between the base and bear cases on a 4-year view** — the most useful bound on the packaging-disruption risk now on this page. **The technical trade-off UBS draws, which is the substance of the debate:** CoPoS "should offer stronger package-level performance through **tighter interconnect, better signal integrity, broader integration options such as 3D stacking (and eventually, CPO)**, and TSMC's execution track record," while EMIB-T's edge is **structural cost via interposer elimination and scalability to ultra-large packages built directly on large substrates**. **TSMC's own execution risks, named: "CoPoS warpage, panel process uniformity, and new equipment readiness"** — and the timing disadvantage is explicit, with **EMIB-T targeted for MP in H2'27-28, slightly AHEAD of CoPoS in 2028.** UBS's conclusion is a share-of-mind concession rather than a share-of-revenue one: **"EMIB-T could be a meaningful second-source for selected hyperscaler ASICs, although CoPoS should remain the preferred platform for customers prioritizing the highest performance and execution reliability."** Also: "CoPoS and EMIB-T should drive another capex cycle, benefiting equipment suppliers" (UBS initiates All Ring at Buy NT$1,550; likes GPTC, Chroma, ASMPT, Gudeng; substrate top picks Ibiden and Unimicron). (UBS, 2026-07-26) |
| 07-25 | AlphaSense expert call — **former Senior Process Engineer, ASM International** (independent consultant) | supply | **bull** | **An equipment-side expert, unprompted, lands on the same conclusion as UBS and adds the reason: the binding constraint is TOOLS, not capital or demand.** Per the call summary: the expert is "**highly confident in the continued dominance and technological leadership of the largest advanced packaging manufacturer, noting that competitors and smaller players face significant barriers to catching up**"; sees demand for **"CoWoS and future CoPoS technologies as outpacing supply for the foreseeable future, with supply constraints driven more by tool availability than by financial or market factors"** (bonding tools named specifically); describes the market as **bifurcated — "one dominant player (TSMC) leading in complex technologies like CoWoS and CoPoS, while OSATs focus on less complex, mature packaging solutions"**; and characterises the **TSMC-NVIDIA relationship as "deeply integrated, giving both parties significant advantages and making it difficult for competitors to break in,"** with NVIDIA securing long-term capacity through advance orders. Expects **"no major technological breakthroughs or disruptive entrants in the near term; the roadmap for advanced packaging is well-defined."** Two things to hold: this is a **2-page expert-network summary, and the expert's background is front-end ALD/process integration rather than OSAT operations**, so treat the "no disruptive entrant" conclusion as a well-informed prior, not a supply-chain audit. It does, however, corroborate the tool-availability framing independently of the sell-side. |
| 07-26 | MS · Derrick Yang / Vivi Huang / Andy Meng ("Progress for Glass in Advanced Packaging") | supply | mixed | **TSMC's glass-core path is dated and it is BEHIND Intel's: "Innolux (3481.TW) is working with TSMC and Ibiden on a glass core substrate project, with mass production seen in 2H28 or 2029, per our checks" — versus Intel/Lens Tech with a pilot line by end-2026 and meaningful volume from 2H27.** Same ordering as EMIB-T vs CoPoS, which is worth noting as a pattern rather than a coincidence: on both bridges and substrates, Intel is ~12-18 months earlier and TSMC is betting on the higher-performance version arriving later. Glass core matters because it addresses **warpage** (CTE matching) — one of the three CoPoS risks UBS names above — plus lower signal loss and greater mechanical strength at panel dimensions. Also in the field: **BOE** (fabless customers in China and North America, capacity 2027, MP 2028, aspiring to the full glass-core ABF process) and **AUO** (glass for LEO antenna modules and micro-LED optical, no MP timetable). MS's caveat: display-chain players are "gaining more traction in the TGV and copper plating processes," but "technical challenges are still there… hurdles are more related to process control instead of pure equipment capability," and "more meaningful revenue contribution should take time." |

| 07-24 | Austin Lyons (Chipstrat) | supply | mixed | **"TSMC could spend $10B more on N2 capacity. Why won't they?"** — an independent-analyst framing of the central capital-discipline question on this page: with N2 demand evidently exceeding supply (super-hot-run mix at 20-30% from 10-20%, each tier at ~+50% ASP), the constraint on capacity is **TSMC's own choice**, not the order book. Directly relevant to two live threads here: (a) the **capex-conservatism** assumption that SemiAnalysis uses to argue **~50k+ WSPM of N3 overflow could flow to Intel** (see [[INTC]]), and (b) the **house sensitivity work** on 2026-28 capex / EUV / the 70% GM bridge already on this page — if TSMC chooses to under-build relative to demand, the GM bridge gets easier and the overflow-to-competitors risk gets larger at the same time. Logged as a framing piece, not new company data. |
| 07-24 | SemiAnalysis · Shan/Nishball/Xie et al. ("Can AMD break the CUDA Moat? AMD Advancing AI 2026") | supply | **bull** | **The strongest leading-edge + packaging datapoint of the run, arriving via the AMD launch teardown — and every superlative in it is a TSMC superlative.** AMD's **MI455X is the first 2nm datacenter silicon to ship** (N2 compute tiles, with the Venice CPU the other early N2 adopter) while **"all of the competing accelerator platforms are on N3"** — a hard, dated confirmation that the N2 datacenter ramp has begun and that TSMC, not a customer, owns the gate. Packaging: the MI455 package is the **largest CoWoS-L module shipping, at 5.5x reticle size**; AMD is **"still the only company adopting TSMC's SoIC-X hybrid bonding,"** which lets it scale in z as well as x/y; and the package totals **3,470 mm² of logic silicon — "by far the most amount of silicon that is being shipped in a single package"** (8 N2 XCDs hybrid-bonded onto 2 reticle-sized base dies + 2 N3P I/O dies, each I/O die carrying 72 flexible lanes across 212G UALoE / 64G Infinity Fabric / PCIe Gen6 / 128G UALink / xGMI4). Base dies are now near reticle-sized with a **32mm HBM-facing edge** to fit 3 HBM4 cubes per edge (vs 29mm on the MI300X AID). **The net-new roadmap item: SemiAnalysis believes MI455 is the first chip to ship with active Local Silicon Interconnect (aLSI).** CoWoS-L bridges have been **passive** to date (wiring + capacitors only); **TSMC's ISSCC 2026 aLSI presentation demonstrated active bridges with a low-power repeater circuit that regenerates signals mid-channel**, so the bridge shares the signal-integrity burden and **"the PHYs on the top dies can shrink meaningfully, at a nearly negligible energy cost, reclaiming leading-edge silicon and shoreline for compute and memory."** The giveaway SemiAnalysis flags: **TSMC's demo "test vehicle" was the MI455 interposer itself** — two base dies, 12 HBM4 stacks, two IO dies. Read-through for this page: aLSI is a *new monetisable layer of packaging value-add inside CoWoS-L*, on top of the reticle-scaling roadmap already tracked here (Arete: CoWoS-L 5.5x '26 → 9.5x '27 → 14x '28 — note MI455 at 5.5x lands exactly on Arete's 2026 mark), and it raises the technical bar Intel's EMIB-T has to clear (SemiAnalysis separately judges EMIB-T "still behind" partly because TSMC is "further along on… active LSI"). Forward: **MI500 (2027) pivots to co-packaged copper (CPC) and near-packaged optics (NPO)** — 9 optical engines at 6.4 Tbit/s uni-di each = 57.6T scale-up, light from ELSFPs — the AMD-side analogue of the COUPE/CPO inflection already on this page. [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_24_ENC_Can_AMD_break_the_CUDA_Moat_AMD_Advancing_AI_2026_email.html) |
| 07-27 | UBS · Sunny Lin / Randy Abrams / Nicolas Gaudois / Timothy Arcuri / Shingo Hirata / Jerry Su / Diana Chang ("Can Intel challenge TSMC's advanced packaging leadership? — Intel's EMIB-T vs TSMC's CoPoS") | supply | **bull** | 🔴 **The full note, now in hand (the 07-26 row above was the same piece via the Outlook preview; only net-new content is logged here). The headline change is the rating mark: UBS Buy, PT NT$3,650** (share price NT$2,350 priced 24 Jul 2026; mkt cap US$1,931,966m; 21.6x/15.8x 2026-27E P/E, 8.0x/5.8x P/BV, ROE 43.4%/42.7%) — **up from the NT$3,400 this page has carried since 06-28.** Comparators in the same Figure-1 table: **[[INTC]] Neutral US$121** (px $100), **[[MEDIATEK]] Buy NT$6,500** (px NT$3,750), **[[AVGO]] Buy US$485** (px $392), Amkor Neutral US$80; UBS **initiates All Ring at Buy NT$1,550** and carries ASE Buy NT$835, ASMPT Buy HK$200, Chroma Buy NT$2,960, Gudeng Buy NT$710, GPTC Buy NT$5,000, Powertech Neutral NT$240, Ibiden Buy ¥20,500, Unimicron Buy NT$1,200. **Share baseline, which the preview row lacked: TSMC runs 80-85% of advanced packaging in 2026-27E** (base-case table 84%/82%/72%/68%/**67%** for 2026-30E; OSATs 16%→20%; Intel 0/0/9%/11%/13%) — so the 65-70%-by-2030 stress-test outcome is a step down from a mid-80s base, with **OSATs taking the larger absolute slice, not Intel.** **CoPoS calendar, dated:** pilot line **installed late Q2 2026** for evaluation/qualification; **first-generation process flow, equipment specs and key suppliers to be finalized by mid-2027**; **310×310mm² panels first, migrating to 510×515mm²**; MP 2028 with **Nvidia's Feynman the expected first client in H2'28-2029**, AMD and ASICs 2029-30. **Two upside options not previously on the page:** (1) **"TSMC's share could further increase if Google's new TPUs (eg. v10) return to CoPoS and adopt CPO integration"** — the v9 EMIB-T defection is not modelled as permanent, and CPO is the pull-back mechanism; (2) **14x-reticle CoWoS** supporting **~10 large compute dies and 20 HBM stacks**, production **2028**, scalable beyond 14x in 2029 — which bounds EMIB-T's "scales better to ultra-large packages" edge. ⚠️ **That die/HBM spec is CoWoS's, not CoPoS's** — UBS: "TSMC has added 14x-reticle CoWoS to its roadmap, with support for around 10 large compute dies and 20 HBM stacks." **And UBS explicitly de-fuses the glass risk:** both CoPoS and EMIB-T reach MP in 2028 **"without requiring glass-core substrates"**; a glass *panel carrier* "would not… constrain CoPoS production or weigh on yields"; glass-*core* substrates are "unlikely to reach mass production before 2029-30E, if not later" — a direct counterweight to the 07-26 MS row above (Innolux/Ibiden MP 2H28-2029 vs Intel/Lens Tech pilot end-2026). **Manufacturing model:** TSMC keeps chip-on-panel in-house, OSATs take chip-on-substrate — **ASE the primary Taiwan partner, Amkor the most likely Arizona on-substrate partner.** **On the other side of the trade, the EMIB-T detail that bears on TSMC:** EMIB-T substrate fabrication yields **~50% vs >80% for HPC ABF substrates**, packaging yields **~90% on trial runs of MediaTek's TPU v9 prototype** (HPC ideally needs high-90s), and a risk UBS names that is contractual rather than technical — **"integration between the foundry (TSMC) and back-end (Intel) processes, as well as yield responsibility"** (who eats the loss when TSMC owns the die and Intel owns the package). Wet-process uniformity is called out as a concrete CoPoS tooling problem: applying chemicals evenly is harder on a square panel than a round wafer. (UBS, 2026-07-27) [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/UBS_on_adv_packaging.html) |
| 07-28 | **Fubon Research · Sherman Shang / Daniel Yang — "Our advanced packaging, CoWoS forecast adjustments; other updates"** (dist. Jefferies; sector Overweight) | supply | mixed | 🔴 **Fubon's CoWoS model refresh, and the news is a genuine CUT to SoIC that this page's other sources point the other way on. CoWoS is maintained and possibly better; SoIC is worse — and the two are causally linked.** **Capacity:** end-2027 total TSMC CoWoS **held at 180kwpm**; end-2028 **220kwpm**, with Fubon flagging **potential upside to its own 2028 number** *"as we see TSMC slowing down its SoIC expansion to prioritize the CoWoS expansion."* **The SoIC cut, verbatim: "Compared to our earlier forecast of 90k capacity in 4Q28, we revise down our capacity expansion forecast to 70k in 4Q28"** — a ~22% cut to the 4Q28 SoIC exit rate. ⚠️ **This contradicts two sources already on this page and the contradiction is worth keeping rather than smoothing: Redburn (Conor O'Mara, 06-23) describes an "aggressive SOIC expansion 2027-28" (each 10,000-unit line needing 15-20 hybrid bonders, new use cases in silicon photonics + Broadcom's next-gen TPU), and Susquehanna (Hosseini, 07-11) has "SoIC ramping meaningfully through 2028." Fubon is now saying the opposite — that SoIC is being deliberately deprioritised to feed CoWoS. If Fubon is right the read-through is negative for the hybrid-bonder chain (Jefferies flags BESI as the most exposed, TSMC being its largest customer) and mildly positive for TSMC's own CoWoS output; if Redburn/SIG are right the SoIC line stands. Not resolvable here — flagged as a live disagreement, not netted.** **Third-party capacity: ASE's FoCoS forecast raised to 50kwpm for 2027 "to support CPU and networking customers' demand"** — up from the 45kwpm-by-end-2027 mark Fubon itself carried on 06-05 (which had already been raised from 30k). Consistent with the offload strategy this page tracks (non-accelerator packaging to ASE/SPIL to free in-house CoWoS). **The wafer series (Figure 2), net-new granularity: TSMC CoWoS total capacity 129k (2023) → 300k (2024) → 675k (2025) → 1,170k (2026) → 1,890k (2027), with shipments 99.6k (2023) and 1,155.6k (2026) — i.e. UTR 77% in 2023 and 99-100% every year since. Growth rates 132.6% / 125.0% / 73.3% / 61.5%.** **2027 customer allocation (Figure 5, % of TSMC CoWoS then wafers): [[NVDA]] 50.0% / 945,000 · [[AVGO]] 22.3% / 421,800 · AWS 10.4% / 196,500 · [[AMD]] 7.0% / 132,300 · [[MEDIATEK]] 7.3% / 137,700 · [[MSFT]] 1.0% / 18,900 · XLNX and [[CSCO]] 0.5% / 9,450 each · Others 1.0% / 18,900.** ⚠️ **These are TSMC-only CoWoS wafers and must be kept distinct from the total-allocation figures Fubon quotes elsewhere in the same note** — NVDA's **total** allocation is **1.03mn in 2027 vs 629k in 2026** (TSMC **plus SPIL**, the SPIL leg supporting Vera CPU), and AMD's **total** rises to **300k**, the majority of the increase coming from **ASE, not TSMC**. **Chip-output/BOM items that bear on TSMC's mix: after NVDA cancelled the Kyber rack and the four-compute-die Rubin Ultra GPU, Fubon models 12 interposers per CoWoS wafer for Rubin and 10 for Rubin Ultra (a smaller-than-feared interposer step-up), lifting NVDA total chip output to 12.4mn in 2027 from 8.2mn in 2026; Rubin's 2026 volume is cut to ~1mn on the thermal redesign + push-out, offset by higher 3Q26 Blackwell, so the 2026 CoWoS chip-output forecast is "largely unchanged."** Demand framing Fubon closes on, relevant to the 2027-pricing thread on this page: **"the token cost is still a more important factor in driving CSP's capex, and we remain positive about AI market demand."** (Fubon "Our advanced packaging, CoWoS forecast adjustments; other updates", 2026-07-28) [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Our_advanced_packaging_CoWoS_forecast_adjustments_other_up.html) |
| 08-03 | **Fubon · Sherman Shang / Arthur (hosted by Jefferies) — "industry highlights & checks update" supply-chain call (PRIMARY TRANSCRIPT)** | supply | **mixed** | 🔴 **THE CoWoS LADDER IS RESTATED LIVE AND UNCHANGED, THE SoIC CUT FINALLY GETS ITS LOCATION AND ITS SECOND CAUSE, AND THE 2027 CAPEX MARK MOVES UP AGAIN — with an unusually candid statement that it may still not be enough.** ⚠️ **Machine (ASR) transcript; garbled names corrected inline on first use — `Fubon` (ASR: "Foobon") · `CoWoS` (ASR: "CoOS"/"COAS") · `SoIC` (ASR: "SOIC") · `ASE` (ASR: "ASC"/"ASP") · `Ibiden` (ASR: "Epident") · `Vera` (ASR: "V-core"/"V-Ra") · `Venice` (ASR: "V-ness") · `SerDes` (ASR: "services") · `Broadcom` (ASR: "Borcom").** **(1) CAPACITY, verbatim and MAINTAINED: "we maintain our earlier forecast that by the end of 2027, TSMC's total CoWoS capacity will reach 180K wafers per month. And for 2028, we currently forecast TSMC's capacity to be at 220K wafers per month" — with the upside flag restated as an OBSERVATION rather than a hope: "in the short term, we observe TSMC is slowing down the ASIC expansion to prioritize CoWoS production. So we see a potential upside to our 2028 number."** So the 180k/220k pair on this page from the 07-28 primary is confirmed unchanged, three days on, from the analyst's own mouth. **(2) ★ NET-NEW — THE SoIC CUT NOW HAS A FAB, A PHASE AND A SECOND CAUSE. Asked directly whether the SoIC reduction is in AP7 and why: "Yes, mainly in AP7 and to be more specific, it's in PHASE TWO AND PHASE THREE. Because for phase two and phase three, they were supposedly to add SoIC capacities there. But I think now TSMC is slowing down the SoIC expansion a little bit. Because it looks like the CPU DEMAND IS ALSO BEING PUSHED OUT… So here they will prioritize the CoWoS production first."** ⚠️ **Two things this changes. First, it supersedes the framing this wiki has carried since 05-27 that "TSMC's new AP7 fab phases are being dedicated to SoIC" — phases 2 and 3 specifically are being slowed. Second, and more important for the read: the cause is NOT purely a CoWoS-priority reallocation. A CPU-demand PUSH-OUT is a demand statement, and it sits awkwardly against the CPU-renaissance thread on this page (C.C. Wei's agentic-AI CPU resurgence, Fubon's own "server-CPU very strong growth 2027+", the Vera/Venice CoWoS pull). Read the two together as timing rather than level — the CPU volume is still coming, it is arriving later than the SoIC capex schedule assumed — but do not smooth the tension away. See [themes/cowos-packaging](themes/cowos-packaging.md).** **(3) ⚠️ CAPEX MOVES AGAIN, AND WITH A CANDID CAVEAT: "TSMC, yeah, they raised their capex in 26 to 60 to 64 billion. And in 27, now we heard TSMC's capex will further increase to maybe between 80 TO 85 BILLION to support the 3 nanometer and the 2 nanometer expansion. I'M NOT SURE AT THE MOMENT, I'M NOT SURE THAT IS ENOUGH TO SUPPORT ALL THE CUSTOMERS DEMAND."** Superseding Fubon's own **US$80bn** FY27 mark from the 07-17 call (and the US$75bn from 07-08) — old values in `## Changelog`. **And the node-supply statement is unambiguous: "in 2027, I think obviously everyone knows, TSMC's capacity supply in both 2 nanometer and 3 nanometer will be VERY, VERY TIGHT."** ⚠️ **A sell-side analyst saying an $80-85bn capex may be insufficient is the strongest form of the pricing-power argument on this page — it is the supply side of the ≥10% 2027 wafer-hike thesis, stated as a doubt rather than a forecast. [[AVGO]] is explicitly kept on TSMC for foundry despite the Samsung 2nm question ("from the foundry side, I think Broadcom will still use TSMC"), so the tightness is not being relieved by a second source.** **(4) CAPACITY IS THE BINDING CONSTRAINT ON GOOGLE, and that is what makes [[INTC]] structural rather than opportunistic: "TSMC, they don't have enough capacity to do that [12-15mn TPUs]… that's why I say Intel's is a must have in 2028." Detail on [[GOOG]] / [[INTC]] / [[MEDIATEK]].** **(5) ★ NET-NEW — TSMC'S OWN POSTURE ON EMIB HAS TWO PARTS AND BOTH ARE NEW TO THIS PAGE: "TSMC in recent comments about Intel's EMIB-T becomes MORE OPEN-MINDED. And we think TSMC has gone through NEGOTIATION WITH BOTH GOOGLE AND MEDIATEK." Separately: "based on our checks, TSMC is also working on THEIR IN-HOUSE EMIB SOLUTION for different chip design. But currently, it is still at a VERY, VERY EARLY STAGE. And there is NO PRODUCTION TIMELINE YET."** ⚠️ **The second item is the competitively interesting one: the incumbent is building an answer to EMIB-T rather than only absorbing the overflow into CoWoS-L/CoPoS. It is far too early to affect the 2028 TPU decision, but it caps the DURATION of Intel's window and is the standing counter to any thesis treating EMIB-T as a permanent second source. The first item — that TSMC negotiated with both Google and MediaTek — is Fubon's inference, not a disclosure, and should be carried as such.** **(6) ON THE OVERBOOKING QUESTION (asked why Google can be supply-constrained while selling TPUs externally), Fubon points straight back at management rather than offering his own view: "TSMC's management already answered this question during the earnings call. So the production plan is based on… discussion, negotiation with all the customers to make sure there is no overbooking, double booking… and the chip won't become inventory. So based on TSMC's management, they are still very positive on the AI demand and they feel the inventory is fine so far. So they are still very aggressive in expanding their capacity and further raise their capex."** A restatement, not a new datapoint — logged because it is the direct answer to the double-booking bear that recurs on this page. **(7) ★ THE COMPETITIVE FRAME FOR ADVANCED PACKAGING, and it is a genuine sequencing argument: ASE is a PARTNER NOT A COMPETITOR TODAY — "I wouldn't say TSMC is ASE's competitor. I think they are more like a partners… because the CoWoS capacity is very very tight, so some customer just go to ASE for the production… I mean in 26 and 27. But in the following years I think [they] will become competitor. Because TSMC they will expand their advanced packaging fab in the US in Arizona later… TSMC and Nvidia already signed the deal. And the business plan is TSMC in their advanced packaging fab in Arizona will still do the front end production, I mean the CoW or CoP. And [outsource] the on-substrate production to [Amkor]. So Amkor is the most likely competitor for ASE, but NOT NOW… in the following years." ⚠️ ASE "currently has no plan to follow TSMC's footprint to build their fab in the US," which is the mechanism that hands Arizona on-substrate work to Amkor.** ⚠️ **Attribution caveat, stated rather than hidden: the OSAT name is rendered by the ASR as "code"/"Cowd" and is read here as `Amkor` — resolved on context (US footprint, Arizona on-substrate work) and corroborated independently by UBS on this page (07-27: "ASE the primary Taiwan partner, AMKOR the most likely ARIZONA on-substrate partner"). Nothing beyond that is asserted.** **(8) Supplier scope, logged because it bears on who benefits from the CoWoS pool: "we talk to the domestic advanced packaging suppliers, such as `"Seasam"` [ASR-garbled Taiwanese adv-packaging supplier, name not resolvable from the transcript], all of them mentioned that ASE IS THE FASTEST GROWING CUSTOMER in expanding capacities," and that supplier is "involved in CoWoS, EMIB, ASE's [FoCoS], and fan out and TSMC'S NEXT GENERATION CoWoS."** ⚠️ **The Jefferies desk relay of this same call renders the name as "C SUN" in the identical sentence (already on [themes/cowos-packaging](themes/cowos-packaging.md)) — plausibly the same company, but not asserted here.** (Fubon · Sherman Shang / Arthur (hosted by Jefferies), "industry highlights & checks update", 2026-08-03) [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_08_03_sherman_update_03_08_26.html) |

| 08-10 | **Bernstein · Mark Li / Edward Hou / Yipin Cai — "TSMC: Higher revenue & capex on CPU; Outperform with 40% upside" (PRIMARY, 19pp)** | guidance | **bull** | 🔴 **PT RAISED: NT$2,780 → NT$3,300 (ADR US$430 → US$554), Outperform reiterated, ~39% upside vs NT$2,380 close (10 Aug). The multiple is UNCHANGED at 20x forward P/E — the entire PT move is estimates, which is the cleanest form of an upgrade to the numbers.** **Estimates (old → new): FY26E EPS NT$101.62 → NT$110.70 (+8.9%); FY27E NT$124.63 → NT$144.85 (+16.2%); FY28E NT$157.4 → NT$188.70 (+19.9%). ADR US$16.07/19.70 → US$17.18/22.48.** Revenue NT$5,457bn / 7,328bn / 9,293bn (+43.3% / +34.3% / +26.8%), i.e. **US$173bn in 2026 (+41% in USD)**, US$232bn '27, US$294bn '28. **Bernstein is above consensus by a widening margin — EPS +13.1% / +18.5% / +29.2% vs cons NT$97.9 / 122.3 / 146.1, and revenue +5.1% / +11.7% / +17.7% vs cons — and says so explicitly: the gap is "more from higher revenue expectation," not margin.** GM 66.1% / 64.5% / 65.7%, OpM 58.3% / 56.6% / 58.0%, **deliberately assumed flattish across 2026-28 "as we consider the higher cost of oversea production"** — so the EPS raise carries no margin heroics. DPS NT$22 / 31 / 46 / 69 (2025-28E); ROE 44%/42%/42%. **★ THE CALL — CPU IS THE THIRD WAVE, AND IT IS A WAFER STORY: "Agentic AI workloads make CPU the recent surprise to TSMC."** Bernstein's CPU-revenue ladder: **US$6-7bn in 2023 (HSD-10% of total) → US$11-12bn '24 → US$15-16bn '25 (~13%) → ~US$25bn '26E → US$high-30s bn in 2027E (mid-teens%, c.16% of total), a 50-60% CAGR this year and next.** Customer mix is the sharper part: **2023 was AMD ~50% with [[AAPL]] / [[AMZN]] / [[INTC]] 10-15% each; by 2025 it is [[AMD]] 35-40% and INTC "a close No. 2" at mid-30s%** — i.e. Intel's outsourcing has become a third of TSMC's CPU book. The third wave widens it again to **[[GOOG]] Axion, [[ARM]]'s AGI CPU for Meta, Graviton5**. ⚠️ **The structural claim worth carrying: "we project CPU will be about as big as XPU in terms of WAFER revenue contribution in 2027"** — XPU still contributes more in total because it drags packaging revenue with it, but on wafers alone the two legs converge. **N2 pulls forward on the same driver: N2 posted its first revenue contribution in 2Q26 at 3% of total wafer revenue — earlier in its life than N3 (3Q23), N5 (3Q20) or N7 (3Q18) — because AMD's Venice CPU and the MI450-series got there first; HSD% of wafer revenue in 2026 rising to c.20% in 2027,** with flagship mobile joining from 3Q26 ([[AAPL]] A20/A20 Pro first, then [[QCOM]] Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 6 and MediaTek Dimensity 9600) and **"some of Intel['s] upcoming CPU Nova Lake" on TSMC N2 too**. Chiplets are the second-order kicker — modern CPUs package compute tiles on the leading node with IO/platform tiles on **N3/N5/N7**, so those nodes see demand too, "and TSMC hiking capex a few weeks ago is for these nodes too." **Capex: US$64bn 2026E (+57% YoY), US$75bn 2027E, US$82bn 2028E** — capex intensity 37% → 32% → 28%, "still on a rising trajectory but at a moderating pace as the shortage of its capacity narrows gradually over time." ⚠️ **Sits BELOW Fubon's US$80-85bn (08-03) and JPM's ~US$78bn for 2027, above Redburn's US$70-75bn — and Bernstein's explanation for the hike is the one that differs most: "TSMC's recent capex hike is primarily for WAFER capacity, and much less so for CoWoS capacity. CoWoS capacity is still being expanded, but AT A PACE PLANNED ORIGINALLY."** The mechanism is that most CPUs do not use advanced packaging at all, and the ones that do are packaged elsewhere — **AMD's Venice by ASE and [[NVDA]]'s Vera by Amkor.** 🔴 **★ NET-NEW AND THE MOST CONSEQUENTIAL ITEM ON THIS PAGE THIS WEEK — BERNSTEIN ARGUES TSMC IS DELIBERATELY CONCEDING COWOS SHARE, AS STRATEGY: "TSMC is still the best CoWoS supplier, but Amkor offering similar services earlier proves this doesn't have a long-term defendable moat. TSMC thus won't build capacity to defend share. Instead, TSMC strategically lets CoWoS proliferate so that its front-end service won't have a downstream bottleneck."** Their CoWoS-equivalent capacity model at year-end (Exhibit 9, **chart read**, k wpm): **TSMC 60 → 130 → 160 → 170 (CY25 / 26E / 27E / 28E); ASE & Amkor 5 → 30 → 60 → 100; Intel EMIB-T (panel capacity converted to CoWoS-wafer-equivalent) 0 → 0 → 25 → 100** — total ~65 → 160 → 245 → 370. TSMC's share of the equivalent pool therefore falls from ~92% to ~46% by 2028E on Bernstein's own numbers. Drivers named: Amkor packaging low-end NVIDIA GPUs, then Vera (Amkor) and Venice (ASE), and **"Intel packaging Google's TPU v9 with EMIB-T… starting late 2027 and more meaningfully in 2028"**, plus [[SAMSUNG]]'s entry via the AVGO 2.3/2.5D deal, which Bernstein calls **"inevitable given the resources that Samsung commands."** **Margin consequence stated plainly: "the margins of CoWoS will be structurally lower, but TSMC's cautious expansion can keep its CoWoS capacity highly utilized, and the higher asset turns can offset the lower margins."** ⚠️⚠️ **THREE LIVE CONFLICTS WITH SOURCES ALREADY ON THIS PAGE, LOGGED RATHER THAN NETTED. (1) CAPACITY LEVEL: Bernstein has TSMC CoWoS at 160k end-2027 and 170k end-2028 versus Fubon's 180k end-2027 and 220k end-2028 (restated live 08-03) and Arete's 235k YE28 — a ~50k wpm / ~25% gap on the 2028 exit rate, and the gap is deliberate on Bernstein's telling (it is the "won't defend share" thesis expressed as capacity). Whoever is right, the direction of the error is asymmetric: Fubon/Arete's number can only be met by capex Bernstein is not modelling. (2) HYBRID BONDING / SoIC: Bernstein says "we expect TSMC to RAPIDLY EXPAND hybrid bonding capacity in the next 2 years" (SoIC is the stated next focus, so packages stay within CoWoS's round-wafer reticle as dies stack in z) — the direct opposite of Fubon's 07-28/08-03 SoIC CUT (4Q28 exit 90k → 70k, located in AP7 phases 2-3). Both are dated within two weeks. (3) CPU DEMAND: Fubon attributes half of that SoIC cut to "the CPU demand is also being pushed out" — Bernstein's entire thesis is that CPU demand is the upside surprise and is accelerating 50-60%. These two cannot both be true, and the pair is now the single most attackable disagreement on this page. Note which way each analyst's own book leans: Fubon is reading the capex schedule of the packaging fab, Bernstein is reading the customer order book.** **Also on the roadmap, consistent with what the page carries: CoPoS (round wafer → rectangular/square panel, to recover the wasted edge area) and CPO ("so that the fast optical link between two small packages can make them be as powerful as a single large package") both named as active pursuits.** **Valuation frame: 18.8x forward P/E vs a 7-year average of 17.5x (+1SD 21.1x), a 14% discount to SOX and 25% to Nasdaq — "the recent pullback presents a good entry point."** (Bernstein · Mark Li / Edward Hou / Yipin Cai, "TSMC: Higher revenue & capex on CPU", 2026-08-10; first published 10 Aug 12:33 UTC) [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/BERN_259272.html) |

Today, in 2026, **it's the bottleneck choking the entire supply chain**."* **O FOSSO, EM CAMADAS — e o valor da peça é ele ser mecânico, não retórico:** (1) **lock-in de PDK**, agravado por **DTCO** — até 7/5nm o fluxo era foundry desenvolve processo → entrega PDK → cliente projeta; em 3nm e abaixo de 2nm isso quebra, com largura de nanosheet e stack de metal decididos conjuntamente no início do projeto, de modo que *"the option to 'switch foundries later' has become **dramatically more constrained**"*; (2) **custo de máscara por nó** (estimativas IBS): **~$2M em 28nm, $15M em 7nm, $40-50M em 5nm, >$100M em 3nm**, com contagem de máscaras de ~40 (28nm) para >80 (7nm), e a maioria dos designs passando por **um ou dois re-spins** de dezenas de milhões cada; (3) **o OIP**, que empacota EDA (Synopsys, Cadence), IP (ARM) e o packaging próprio (CoWoS, SoIC) num fluxo de referência validado primeiro na TSMC — *"Intel 18A ran into exactly this problem, where IP validation delays slowed customer adoption"*; (4) **o gap de capex** — *"TSMC's annual capex for 2026 is estimated at **$52 to $56 billion**. Intel's is **$16 billion**. TSMC is building more than three Intels every year"*; (5) **o fosso de CONFIANÇA, que é o argumento mais durável e o mais difícil de replicar:** a TSMC é pure-play e não desenha chip nenhum, logo *"it **never competes with its customers**"* — contra Samsung (Exynos vs Snapdragon) e Intel (CPUs próprios), onde *"handing over critical IP to a direct competitor is a fundamentally uncomfortable arrangement"*; (6) talento e cultura, com o texto notando que *"in manufacturing, **culture becomes yield, and yield becomes money**"* e citando os choques culturais reportados na fab do Arizona. **CAPACIDADE E PACKAGING, os números:** 3nm mirando **>200kwpm no fim de 2026 e ~250kwpm em 2027**; produção em volume de 2nm iniciada no **Fab 22 em Kaohsiung no 4T25**; **CoWoS de ~35k painéis/mês no fim de 2024 para 110-125k no fim de 2026, com algumas estimativas em 170k no fim de 2027**, e CoWoS-L, SoIC e **CoPoS** apontando para a manutenção da liderança no roadmap de packaging depois de 2027. **ALOCAÇÃO, e o texto atribui a estimativas de analistas:** *"**NVIDIA holds over 60% of CoWoS-L capacity**, and **Apple has locked in more than half of early 2nm allocation**."* **A FORMA DO GARGALO:** *"TSMC's advanced nodes are **sold out through 2028**. Securing 2nm capacity requires booking a year in advance. **3nm is no longer accepting new project inquiries**; TSMC is steering customers toward 2nm instead."* E o gargalo transborda — PCBs e componentes de laser também no limite, com o próprio Ramachandran apontando fabricantes de PCB de Taiwan e da China em capacidade máxima. **TARIFA:** acordo EUA-Taiwan estabelecendo **15%** sobre chips importados de Taiwan, com a TSMC obtendo alívio em troca de compromisso de **>US$165bn de investimento nos EUA**; quem constrói fab nos EUA importa livre de tarifa até **2,5× a capacidade de novas instalações e 1,5× a das existentes**; tarifas de 25% já impostas em janeiro de 2026 sobre chips específicos de IA (H200 e MI325X citados). ⚠️ **Vintage de abril, autoral e não verificado; vários números (capex 2026, CoWoS, capacidade 3nm) foram desde então superados pelos marks vivos desta página — está aqui como âncora histórica e como o desenho do fosso, não como estimativa adotada** (DAMNANG "The Age of the TSMC Bottleneck", 2026-04-06) |

Recently, **some CoWoS-related processes and comparatively simpler RDL structures have begun flowing to OSATs**… the scope of work OSAT receives is gradually widening."* E há um segundo vetor, que não é de capacidade: *"hyperscalers have begun **voluntarily diversifying their supply chains**. NVIDIA, Google, and Amazon all want to reduce their **100% dependence on TSMC as a single source**. Once an OSAT is qualified for CoWoS-related processes, customers **allocate volume to OSAT directly, separate from what TSMC hands off**."* ⚠️ **É esse o item que corta contra esta página: parte do outsourcing não é overflow temporário, é gestão de risco de cliente e tende a não voltar.** Os OSATs nomeados no texto (ASE, Amkor) **não têm página neste wiki** e ficam em texto simples (DAMNANG "OSAT: The Final Bottleneck in AI Chips", 2026-05-11) |

Não supersede nenhum número desta página.** | **produto / supply** | **mixed (direção clara, timeline não)** | ★ **A razão de o painel existir é geométrica:** o reticle limit é ~26×33mm (~858mm²) e os aceleradores já passaram disso há tempo, então *"TSMC's package area roadmap moves from **5,5× the reticle to 9,5× and then 14×**. At 9,5×, that comes to roughly **8.000 square millimeters**, a square about 9 centimeters on each side"* — e wafer redondo desperdiça borda, painel quadrado não. **Formatos e cronograma (do texto de 16-jun):** primeiro formato de painel **310×310mm**, com roadmap citado passando por **515×510mm** até **750×620mm**; **linha piloto na VisEra (subsidiária da TSMC com experiência em processamento de painel vinda de óptica em nível de wafer para sensores de imagem) em 2026**, produção experimental pequena citada para **2027**, produção em massa **2028-29** na **fase 4 do campus AP7 em Chiayi**, e **a NVIDIA citada como primeiro cliente**. ⚠️ **O freio vem da própria empresa:** *"TSMC Chairman **C.C. Wei** nailed it down: **mass production takes another two to three years and there are no shortcuts**"* — e parte da indústria fala em **2029-2030**. **A INCERTEZA QUE IMPORTA NÃO É SE, É ONDE:** a TSMC divulgou validação de substrato de vidro com **Ibiden e Innolux**, mas *"what TSMC validated… was **not an interposer** but a **0,8mm glass core substrate**, and the program was even named **'glass substrate for CoWoS'**"* — ou seja, ainda não está decidido se o vidro entra como interposer ou como core substrate, e na leitura de junho *"the glass whose spot is currently most solid is **on the carrier side**"* (check de indústria do Ming-Chi Kuo: **carrier de vidro de 310×310mm**, com estrutura de painel de 510×515mm virando core substrate flutuada para o estágio de volume). Barreira física citada repetidamente: gestão de **CTE** quando o vidro cai para ~400μm como interposer, e **warpage piorando conforme o painel cresce**. ⚠ E o jogo não é só da TSMC — *"**Intel and Samsung are each pushing panel packaging and glass in their own way**, so even which camp and which method wins is still open."* **Quem monetiza primeiro, na leitura do autor:** inspeção e metrologia, porque *"they are needed on **any** path, whether CoWoS, CoPoS, or CoWoP"* e o equipamento entra **antes** do volume, já na fase de validação (DAMNANG "Glass Substrate: The Order the Money Flows In", 2026-06-13; "If CoPoS Arrives, Who Makes Money First", 2026-06-16) |
